For everyone who needs it . <h> Irish drivers are borrowing more to buy cars . Why ? And what are the risks ? <p> IRISH CONSUMERS ARE taking on an increasing amount of debt to buy cars . <p> Central Bank figures on hire-purchase agreements and ' personal contract plans ' ( PCPs ) -- a similar type of finance arrangement that allows you to drive a car you do not own in return for monthly payments -- show the value of outstanding loans has climbed in the past two years . <p> The amount borrowed by consumers on PCPs increased by 20% to ? 2.17 billion between January 2024 , when the Central Bank started collecting data , and June 2025 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can be used for various products but in practice are overwhelmingly used for cars -- increased by 8% to ? 3.2 billion over the same period . <p> PCPs have been available in Ireland for almost 20 years , since Volkswagen first began offering them . These contracts offer lower monthly repayments than hire-purchase but require an upfront deposit or trade-in and , if you want to keep and actually own the car , a large final " balloon payment " . <p> In practice many people give the car back and start another PCP rather than make this payment , explained Paddy Comyn , head of automotive content and communications with DoneDeal Cars . <p> " In effect , they 're pretty much leasing the car , because they 're just paying a monthly figure , they 're having the use of this car , they hand it back and go again , " Comyn said . <p> That suits the manufacturer , because they get another unit gone . They 're getting a car back that 's in good condition , it 's been serviced with the dealership @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they will make some money on the servicing . <p> " A lot of people will be on their second , third or fourth PCP at this stage . " <p> Hire-purchase means you pay an amount each month to hire the car and only become the legal owner when you have made the final payment . <h> What 's behind the increase ? <p> There are a few reasons why drivers are borrowing more for cars , including that cars have become more expensive . When PCPs first came on the market in Ireland almost 20 years ago , a Volkswagen Golf cost about ? 20,000 , whereas today the same model is closer to ? 40,000 . <p> " The cars that are selling now in big numbers -- hybrids , electrics -- these are all more expensive machinery , " Comyn said . <p> " Some brands will report that 70% of their new cars would be PCP , " Comyn said . <p> PCPs have changed the market for new cars in Ireland , allowing a new set of drivers to access new cars who would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ surged 30% to more than 120,000 units in 2015 , driven partly by economic recovery and partly by the increased availability of finance , and have remained at about that level since , motor industry data shows . <p> PCPs are very popular because they allow people to drive a brand-new car for much lower monthly repayments than they would make on hire-purchase . <p> The annual percentage rate ( APR ) , or yearly cost of borrowing , on many PCP and hire-purchase contracts is lower than high street banks or credit unions typically offer , and sometimes as low as 0% . <p> Comyn believes some manufacturers have been trying to push consumers back towards traditional hire-purchase because this means the consumer rather than the company takes on the risk associated with the uncertain future value of electric vehicles . <p> " At the end of the day , if I bought an electric car on PCP and the value of it plummets , I can just hand the car back and walk away . The risk is with the lender , not with the customer , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and buy that car in cash , the risk is all with you -- and the same if you hire-purchase it , and then you 're left with this thing which has n't retained the value you thought it might . " <p> For example , Volkswagen is offering 0% APR on hire-purchase for a new Polo worth just under ? 25,000 and a 5.9% rate on PCP . <p> Hire-purchase would typically cost ? 359 per month for four years , on top of an ? 8,600 deposit . PCP would cost ? 239 per month for three years , on top of a deposit of ? 7,700 , plus a balloon payment of ? 12,000 at the end of the term to keep the car. * <p> Comyn has found himself in the situation of having a PCP on an electric car that has dropped in value . It was valued at ? 30,000 when he bought it , but the manufacturer dropped the price by ? 10,000 a few weeks later . Under the terms of the contract , he would have to pay ? 14,500 to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but he could buy the same car second-hand for ? 6,000- ? 7,000 . <p> " The only option to me is to hand that car back , which is exactly what I 'll do , " Comyn said . <h> ' Real risks ' <p> The Competition and Consumer Protection Commission ( CCPC ) said PCPs are " one of the least flexible forms of credit " . <p> " Rules around reasonable condition and certain mileage limits can apply to the contract and may impact the final payment if these conditions are not met , " the CCPC said . <p> It urged consumers to check the interest rate and read the fine print . It urged consumers to plan for the final payment which can be " very large " . <p> " The biggest risk when it comes to PCPs is the final balloon payment at the end of the contract . Consumers can often assume that they will trade in their car at that point , which might cover the balloon payment and a deposit for a new PCP contract . <p> " However , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and there are lots of things that could happen to your car , like damage , before that point . <p> " You do n't want to find yourself in negative equity on the car and facing into a substantial final payment bill . " <p> The CCPC said that if consumers run into difficulties with a PCP arrangement they may be offered the option to voluntarily surrender the car , but they should not agree to this without looking at other options . <p> For active PCPs , the proportion of overdue loans is low at just 0.06% , but for hire-purchase it is higher at 0.8% , according to the Central Bank 's figures . These figures have remained stable over time . <p> The CCPC said consumers who can not repay their car finance should try to use a legal provision known as the ' half rule ' . <p> " Under the law you have the right to return a car bought with PCP or hire-purchase once you 've paid half the price of the car , and you wo n't have to pay the rest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ returning your car under the half rule , do not sign a voluntary surrender form . This is likely to be extremely expensive , and you 'll end up owing far more money than with the half rule . " <p> The increased accessibility of new cars has helped to lower transport emissions and got people into safer cars , Comyn said . <p> However , it was raised at the Environmental Protection Agency 's annual climate change conference this week that government policy encouraging uptake of electric vehicles is seeing many people roll over one PCP into the next , meaning a new car every three years -- but the emissions involved in the manufacture of these vehicles are not being accounted for here in Ireland . In response , the EPA said it is funding research on calculating consumption-based emissions . <p> *The number of years in this paragraph has been corrected from an earlier version . <p> Readers like you are keeping these stories free for everyone ... <p> A mix of advertising and supporting contributions helps keep paywalls away from valuable information like this article . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support us with a monthly payment or a once-off donation . <p> The Journal supports the work of the Press Council of Ireland and the Office of the Press Ombudsman , and our staff operate within the Code of Practice . @@137743450 <h> Oh no ! George Lee says scientists fear that rising temperatures may become new normal <p> Europe is the fastest-warming continent on Earth , with temperatures rising at roughly twice the global average rate . <p> Climate change is loading the dice . Warmer oceans , altered atmospheric circulation and a steadily rising global temperature baseline mean that when favourable weather patterns develop , they now produce more extreme outcomes than they once did , reports RTE . <p> That is precisely what happened during this week 's extraordinary mini-heatwave . <p> For 29 years , Ireland 's May temperature record stood at 28.4C , set at Ardfert , Co Kerry , on 31 May 1997 , reports RTE . <p> Records are normally broken by tiny margins -- perhaps 0.1C or 0.2C . Instead , Ireland 's long-standing May record was exceeded by more than two degrees . <p> The first sign came at Shannon Airport , where temperatures reached 28.6C . Twenty-four hours later , the same location recorded 30.5C , reports RTE . <p> Such a leap is remarkable . Climate scientists say an event of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of human-caused climate change . <p> Ireland was not alone . Across the UK , long-standing May temperature records were also broken . At Kew Gardens in London , temperatures reached 34.8C before climbing to 35.1C the following day . The old UK May record of 32.8C had stood since 1922 , reports RTE . <p> New May records were also set in Wales and Northern Ireland . In Cardiff , temperatures jumped from 27.4C on Monday to 32.9C on Tuesday . In Co Fermanagh , Thomastown reached 24.5C . <p> The UK Met Office said such temperatures would be exceptional even in midsummer , let alone in May , reports RTE . <p> What made the event especially striking was not simply the intensity of the heat , but its timing . <p> The heatwave arrived while Europe was still in meteorological spring . Heat dome events are normally associated with the height of summer , particularly July and August . They are rarely seen in May , reports RTE . <p> Peter Thorne , Director of the ICARUS Climate Research Centre at Maynooth University , described the temperatures @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The event was driven by a powerful heat dome . Warm air moving northwards from Africa became trapped beneath a strong area of high pressure over western Europe . As the air sank , it was compressed and heated further , allowing temperatures to soar across a wide area , reports RTE . <p> TheLiberal.ie wo n't quitPlease support us with a small donation on PayPal ! <p> At least nine people died in drowning and water-related incidents in the UK . World number one tennis player Jannik Sinner was forced out of the French Open in Paris after suffering dizziness and lethargy in temperatures approaching 33C , reports RTE . <p> These impacts highlight a reality that is often overlooked . Early-season heat can dry soils , warm coastal waters , increase wildfire risk , place pressure on infrastructure and lead to elevated night-time temperatures that prevent people and buildings from cooling down . London experienced precisely such conditions last week when temperatures remained above 20C overnight in a rare tropical night , reports RTE . <p> Met ? ireann warns that prolonged heat can pose risks for people with chronic illnesses , children and outdoor workers . Just this month , Accor hotels launched a five-year sustainability innovation programme . This is likely to be related to the EPA 's role in reporting on emissions as well as water-quality issues . <p> Astonishingly , only one in eight farmers reports trusting the Government on climate change , far lower than the national average . <p> Among the wider public , misconceptions as to what constitutes effective climate behaviour are widespread . What is clear from the new poll is that the gap between the intensifying global climate crisis and public concern is still very wide , with little sign of closing , due in no small part , I would suggest , to mixed and confused messaging from our politicians . <p> John Gibbons is an environmental journalist and author of The Lie of the Land : A game plan for Ireland in the climate crisis

@@137752166 <h> Will clock restart for emissions on non-EU flights ? <p> Aviation sector has set out plan to reach net-zero under its Destination 2050 roadmapAirTeamImages <p> This summer 's review by the European Commission on the effectiveness of CORSIA and whether to extend the scope of its EU ETS scheme reignites arguments on how best to tackle aviation 's emissions . <p> Almost 14 years after the European Union put a pause on plans to apply its emissions trading system ( ETS ) to flights in and out of the bloc , the issue this summer returns to the spotlight . <p> The European Commission agreed to " stop-the-clock " on applying EU ETS on flights to third countries as part of the political brinksmanship which eventually resulted in agreement for the carbon offsetting and reduction scheme for international aviation ( CORSIA ) at the 2013 ICAO General Assembly . <p> That deferral remains in place until the end of this year and , ahead of its next move , the European Commission is carrying out a planned review of the effectiveness of CORSIA and EU ETS . The Commission @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to follow later that month . <p> As part of this review , the Commission is also evaluating whether to expand the scope of the scheme to cover non- CO2 emissions , which notably includes the potential warming effects from persistent contrails . <p> This has prompted fresh arguments from the industry and environment groups on whether the European ETS scheme should be expanded in scope . <h> Environmental concerns <p> After an initial pilot period , the first phase of CORSIA began in 2024 and runs until the end of this year . Participation during these two stages has been voluntary and as of the start of 2026 there were 130 participating states . That does not include major aviation markets like Brazil , China , India and Russia . <p> The mandatory phase of CORSIA , which will encompass all large aviation markets , begins from the start of next year running through to 2035 , with offsetting requirements applying to emissions above the baseline level set at 85% of 2019 levels . <p> Environmental campaigners , highlighting the lack of tax on aviation fuel and continued increased @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ point to the lack of tangible reductions from CORSIA so far and the limited impact of EU ETS because it does not cover third-country flights . <p> " It 's been 14 years and what we have had is CORSIA , but essentially no emissions have actually been reduced under that yet . All we 've lost is 14 years , " says Aoife O'Leary , founder and chief executive of Opportunity Green , an NGO focused on sectors where emissions are not yet falling . <p> " The science is just getting clearer and clearer , and the idea that in 2026 we would say to people , ' do n't worry about it , you can fly from Brussels to New York , and we wo n't even ask you to account for that pollution ' , that is what is at stake here , " she adds . <p> " It 's whether we actually as Europeans believe that climate change is real and is something we should tackle or not , " she says . " So , I ca n't really understate the importance of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ groups , highlights the missed opportunity from excluding extra-European Economic Area ( EAA ) flights from EU ETS . It cites data showing that excluding these flights has led to over 1 billion tonnes of CO2 going unregulated since 2012 . Opportunity Green argues extending EU ETS would lead to only a 2-6% average increase in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the risk of carbon leakage remains minimal . It is concentrated on specific flights , and that is actually mostly those between Europe and Asia . Even there , there is only a very small cost advantage to splitting up that flight rather than taking the direct flight , " he adds . " So , in general , this risk has been overstated . " <p> Opportunity Green argues that if the exemption on international aviation from EU ETS is not removed , ? 124 billion will go uncollected between 2027 and 2040 , and 1.2 billion tonnes of CO2 will be left unregulated . <h> Growing sector <p> Lobby group Transport &amp; Environment says aviation remains the EU 's fastest-growing source of emissions -- in contrast to agriculture , manufacturing and some other transport modes that have cut their greenhouse gases . <p> It in May published analysis showing emissions from flights departing from airports in Europe last year surpassed pre-pandemic levels for the first time -- emitting 195 Mt of CO2 for flights departing Europe in 2025 , a 2% increase compared to pre-Covid levels . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ clear signal that the industry has no intention of cleaning up its act , " says Giacomo Miele , lead author and analyst of the analysis . @@137752536 <h> Event details and registration15 July 2026 , 15.00 -- 16.00 BST <p> The oceans are Earth 's largest natural buffer , acting as a vast carbon sink that absorbs around a quarter of the carbon dioxide generated by human activity . These vast expanses of water also serve as a thermal regulator , soaking up excess heat trapped by greenhouse gases and distributing it across the globe , helping to stabilise our climate . <p> However , over two centuries of increasing human impact , the buffering capacity of this vital resource is being stretched . Rising carbon dioxide levels are driving changes in ocean chemistry and temperature , with far-reaching consequences -- not only for marine ecosystems , but also for the health of the planet as a whole . <p> At a time when climate change is accelerating , monitoring the oceans ' biogeochemical cycles has never been more critical . In this webinar , we will hear from two researchers who are tracking ocean health through its chemistry , offering vital insights into what changing seas mean for our future . <p> The relationship between plankton @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ change <h> Mariana Bif <p> Mariana Bif is an assistant professor at the University of Miami 's Rosenstiel School of Marine , Atmospheric , and Earth Science , US . Her research uses autonomous robotic platforms such as Biogeochemical-Argo floats alongside multiplatform observations to investigate how extreme climate events affect marine ecosystems . She studies how marine heatwaves and low-oxygen conditions alter food webs , the biological carbon pump , nitrogen cycling and ocean chemistry throughout the global ocean . Her work aims to better understand how changing ocean conditions reshape marine biogeochemical cycles . <h> Clare Ostle <p> Clare Ostle is a marine biogeochemist asking what plankton can tell us about the changing ocean . Her work bridges plankton ecology , biogeochemistry and observation technology , focusing on how plankton communities shape carbon cycling and the ocean 's role in climate regulation . She combines long-term observations with sensor development to detect biological change , with a particular interest in expanding the Continuous Plankton Recorder ( CPR ) as a monitoring platform . @@137773524 <h> As temperatures rise and heatwaves become more frequent , gardening experts are recommending drought-resistant plants as climate-resilient alternatives to hydrangeas <p> Gardeners are being warned to avoid planting one stunning flower in their gardens , despite its beautiful colours , as it is being destroyed by our rising temperatures . <p> ? <p> Due to climate change and increasing temperatures in many parts of the world , many plants that once flourished with ease are beginning to falter . This is particularly true for plants known to rely on consistent moisture and cooler temperatures , which are now becoming dehydrated during prolonged hot dry spells with scarce rainfall . <p> In hot , sunny weather conditions , water evaporates quickly from the soil 's surface . In some cases , this means it disappears before plants have a chance to absorb it . <p> Signs your hydrangea may be suffering heat stress include appea leaves appearing scorched and twisted , and petals turning brown rather than their previously brilliant colours . Similarly , drooping branches is also a strong indication that your flower is distressed reports the Mirror . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ towards more drought-tolerant alternatives that still deliver a vibrant splash of colour while demanding far less water . <p> Popular choices include lavender , ceanothus , and Russian sage , all of which boast stunning blooms and require significantly less maintenance , as well as beautiful flowers like lantanas and blanket flowers , which are both robust and colourful . <p> Another advantage of planting drought-tolerant plants is that many are perennials . This means once you 've purchased them , you can enjoy their beauty for years , unlike seasonal bedding plants . <p> Article continues below <p> For added convenience , gardeners are also encouraged to plant drought-resistant bulbs in traditionally cooler and wetter areas , helping to create a more resilient outdoor space . <p> These can include autumn-blooming perennials and carefully timed spring plantings , ideally established before the summer heatwaves arrive . <p> At Reach and across our entities we and our partners use information collected through cookies and other identifiers from your device to improve experience on our site , analyse how it is used and to show personalised advertising . @@137783506 <p> AI backlash is growing . However , if you listen to state and industry boosters , you 'd think the fate of the world rests on AI . To them , AI will solve everything -- including the climate impacts of the industry 's own data centre expansion , which requires immense land , energy , and resources . <p> This techno-optimism feels like gaslighting . How can these resource-hungry technologies solve problems of their own making ? <p> Beyond environmental impacts , AI data centres are inflating a financial bubble . Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella explained in Davos 2026 that the AI " boom " will bust without society-wide adoption . According to a recent article in The Economist , the major cloud providers are pumping $800bn into data centre infrastructure , driving them into major losses . <p> You can see why tech elites -- and the states and geopolitical blocs that have bet their economies on this technology -- are concerned . US military actions , resource grabs , and tariffs victimise allies and adversaries alike -- including Europe . <p> While major European powers have acted on this in recent attempts , for example , to move French government services away from US platforms , the reality is that even operating systems and cloud platforms are undergirded by the US stack . <p> Ireland is especially vulnerable . In this context , the viability of the Irish economy is inextricable from our geopolitical alignments . <p> Ireland 's FDI-led development model already means that public infrastructure is developed towards FDI needs . <p> Ireland 's history of postcolonial under-development and public policy designed for attracting FDI has made infrastructure anachronistic and imbalanced -- high-tech energy systems for multinational data centres exist alongside a creaking electricity grid and stalled energy transition . <p> The Government 's blinkered enthusiasm for AI and its infrastructures can only be explained by the context of EU industrial strategy and our national history of US FDI lock-in -- infrastructure and energy must be for economy and security , rather than for the people and collective flourishing . <p> The economic interests of AI companies become conflated with Ireland 's national interests . <p> Political economist Ilias Alami argues that geopolitical competition around AI encourages an " irrational use and allocation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ grow consumer demand . <p> A more environmentally just and even rational industrial policy would privilege meaningful adaptation to climate change , supporting economic activities that directly benefit people in Ireland -- not those that are designed to automate jobs , hoover up resources , keep us way too online , or commit genocide . <p> Lawmakers must reconsider the policy imperatives inserting these undemocratic and environmentally destructive technologies into our economies and public life . <p> The growing popular rejection of AI represents a struggle over the future of economic development in a rapidly warming and increasingly volatile world . <p> Patrick Brodie is an assistant professor in the School of Information and Communication Studies at University College Dublin .

Motorists are led by their pockets , as evidenced by the changes to the tax regime from engine size to emissions-based back in 2008 and past scrappage @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ discount promotions via similar schemes . A first-come , first-served structure is likely to benefit a buyer who is ready to make the change anyway . The motorist who needs time to consider the lifestyle changes and charging options , and to sort out finance will miss out . <p> Where the pilot scheme will likely succeed is in planting the seed in motorists ' heads to consider moving to electric , and what that might mean to their daily lives . It also distracts from the Government 's decision to lower the price cap for the current EV grant .

@@137804758 <h> UN Warns Of Urgent Climate Warning As World Braces For Extreme Heat <p> Last week , India hit highs of 48C for over a week , making daily life nearly impossible . Sudarshan Jha , Shutterstock <p> The United Nations has issued an urgent climate warning as a developing El Ni ? o event threatens to push global temperatures even higher and increase the risk of extreme weather around the world . <p> The World Meteorological Organization ( WMO ) has confirmed that unusually warm waters in the tropical Pacific Ocean are driving the development of a significant El Ni ? o event , with forecasters warning it is likely to intensify in the months ahead . <p> According to the WMO , there is an 80% chance that above-average temperatures will be recorded across most of the globe between June and August , while there is a 90% likelihood that El Ni ? o conditions will persist and strengthen through November and beyond . <p> Responding to the latest forecasts , UN Secretary-General Ant ? nio Guterres said the world must treat the findings as an urgent warning @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is an urgent climate warning , " he said , adding that El Ni ? o conditions will " pour fuel on the fire of a warming world . " <p> He warned that the impacts would travel farther , hit harder and cross borders with increasing speed . <p> El Ni ? o is one of the most powerful naturally occurring climate patterns on Earth . It occurs when ocean surface temperatures in the central and eastern equatorial Pacific become significantly warmer than normal . The phenomenon typically develops every two to seven years and can last between nine and 12 months . <p> While scientists say there is no evidence that climate change is increasing the frequency or strength of El Ni ? o events , the additional warming caused by greenhouse gas emissions can amplify their effects , leading to more severe heatwaves , droughts and heavy rainfall . <p> Andrea Celeste Saulo , Secretary-General of the WMO , said countries should prepare for a potentially strong event . <p> " We need to prepare for a potentially strong El Ni ? o , which will exacerbate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ both on land and in the ocean , " she said . <p> Meteorologists believe 2027 could become the hottest year ever recorded as the warming influence of El Ni ? o combines with long-term climate change trends . The previous El Ni ? o event , which spanned 2023 and 2024 , was among the five strongest on record and contributed to record-breaking global temperatures . <p> The impacts of El Ni ? o vary around the world . It typically brings increased rainfall to parts of South America , the southern United States , the Horn of Africa and Central Asia , while creating drier conditions across Australia , Indonesia , Central America , the Caribbean and parts of southern Asia . <p> For Ireland , the outlook is somewhat mixed . <p> WMO projections suggest rainfall levels could be lower than normal as El Ni ? o develops , while temperatures are expected to remain above average . However , Ireland is not expected to experience the most severe warming impacts associated with the event . <p> The warning comes as much of Europe experiences increasingly intense @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Portugal approached 40C , while the UK recorded its hottest May day on record with temperatures reaching 34.8C in London . <p> Authorities in France also reported deaths linked to the heatwave and issued multiple weather alerts as extreme temperatures swept across the country . <p> Climate scientists say these events are consistent with a broader warming trend . <p> Data from the World Meteorological Organization and the European climate service Copernicus show that Europe has warmed approximately twice as fast as the global average since the 1980s , making heatwaves more frequent and more intense . <p> The latest warning also comes as Ireland faces growing pressure over its own climate commitments . <p> New projections from the Environmental Protection Agency ( EPA ) indicate that Ireland is on track to significantly miss its legally binding emissions targets for 2030 . @@137816972 <h> At a Time of Climate Crisis : Ireland has clean energy targets but no strategy for ending oil and gas <p> Ireland has climate targets and clean energy targets . What it lacks is a plan to end dependence on fossil fuels . This is the perfect moment to develop one , for at least three reasons . <p> First , the past number of months and years have made it painfully clear that fossil fuels are not just a problem for the climate . Our dependence on fossil fuels has been central both to recent energy insecurity fears , and to the cost-of-living crisis triggered by wars in which oil and gas supply became instruments of geopolitical power . Energy security used to be framed around securing fossil fuel supplies , but there 's increasing recognition that real energy security means reducing dependence on them altogether . <p> Ireland will cohost a big international conference on Transitioning Away from Fossil Fuels next year . At the most recent UN climate summit , Cop30 in Brazil , Ireland supported a call for a roadmap away from fossil fuels , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ International Court of Justice advisory opinion on climate change , also backed by Ireland . <p> The final Cop30 agreement failed to even mention fossil fuels explicitly , as powerful fossil fuel-producing countries continue to resist language that directly acknowledges the primary cause of climate change . But there is no obvious reason why Ireland , which exports virtually no fossil fuels , should allow itself to be held back by countries whose politics is tied to fossil fuel extraction . <p> International commitments and ambitious diplomacy are important , but domestic implementation of a fossil fuel exit strategy is where Ireland can show important leadership . <p> Third , and critically , the Government is preparing a new national energy strategy . This is the ideal opportunity for an explicit plan for a managed exit from fossil fuels . <p> Ireland already has a Climate Action Plan that sets out targets for renewable electricity , electric vehicles and retrofits . But clean energy targets alone are not enough . <p> We have targets for what to build , and high-level commitments to climate neutrality , but still no coherent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fuels still account for nearly 80 per cent of Ireland 's energy mix . This is the missing link in Ireland 's energy policy . <p> The absence of such a plan creates several risks . <p> We can simultaneously expand clean technologies while continuing to lock ourselves into fossil fuel dependence , contrary to climate and energy security objectives . Data centres , for example , may grow with the promise of being powered by renewables in the long term , but in the short term be powered by gas . EV sales may grow , but oil demand may remain stubbornly high if sales of fossil fuelled vehicles do not decline . <p> The absence of a plan also creates ambiguity that allows fossil fuel infrastructure to continue expanding under the banner of " transition " or " energy security " , increasing the risk of stranded assets and greenwashing . <p> A fossil fuel phase-out plan would close those loopholes . <p> What would such a plan actually look like ? <p> First , it would need to foreground a just transition to gain legitimacy . Equity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ energy poverty can be a cornerstone of the energy strategy , alongside plans to support workers and communities affected by the transition away from fossil fuels . <p> Second , it would need to establish clear , quantitative and time-bound trajectories for reducing oil , coal and fossil gas consumption , with transition pathways and intermediate milestones for each sector . " Net-zero by 2050 " is a necessary target , but insufficient as a roadmap : climate commitments and energy security both demand a much faster transition . <p> Importantly , the main barriers here are no longer technological , or even about cost . The real barriers are electricity infrastructure , institutional capacity and mandate , finance , planning systems , market structures , public perception , and lobbying by vested interests . <p> Third , the plan would need to distinguish between temporary fossil fuel use as a backup measure during the clean energy transition , and the continued dependence on fossil fuels embedded in long-lived infrastructure decisions . <p> There may well be limited roles for fossil gas during the transition , particularly while storage and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ But temporary measures have a habit of becoming permanent unless exit pathways are explicitly defined . <p> That is why the continued expansion of fossil fuel infrastructure through LNG , for example , sits uneasily alongside Ireland 's climate obligations . A credible phase-out plan would also force a long-overdue conversation about the future of Ireland 's gas network . <p> Recent interventions from the Irish Academy of Engineering have presented the continued use of fossil fuels beyond 2050 as an engineering inevitability , rather than a judgment about policy ambition , infrastructure choices and institutional willingness to change . Some of the academy 's proposed solutions -- returning to coal , expanding LNG infrastructure and renewed oil and gas exploration -- would deepen rather than reduce fossil fuel dependence . ( Several members of the academy have publicly distanced themselves from these recommendations ) . <p> In some cases , fossil fuel use may be genuinely remain difficult to fully eliminate . But acknowledging niche uses is very different from assuming broad and indefinite dependence . <p> Energy security itself is being reframed : fossil fuel dependence is becoming @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ source insecurity . <p> Now , while the pain of the energy crisis is fresh in the public 's minds , and while Ireland is set to take the stage internationally , is the ideal time to place that reality at the centre of national policy . <p> Hannah Daly is professor of sustainable energy at University College Cork 
@@137816982 <h> Yes , AI could steal your job -- the real question is : should it ? <h> It 's as if everyone is holding their breath and waiting for what they have been told is coming but , as Pope Leo says , we all have a responsibility to shape the future <p> ' Will I have a job tomorrow ? Will the market crash ? Why does OpenAI need a bunker ? Do I need a bunker ? ' Photograph : iStock <p> Sarah O'Connor <p> Thu Jun 04 2026 - 05:00 * 11 MIN READ <p> When an MIT professor called Joseph Weizenbaum created one of the first chatbots in the mid-1960s , he received two shocks in quick succession . The first was how readily people anthropomorphised the rudimentary program , which he called Eliza . " What I had not realised is that extremely short exposures to a relatively simple computer program could induce powerful delusional thinking in quite normal people , " he wrote in his 1976 book Computer Power and Human Reason . <p> But something else troubled him even more : how @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do one day , without pausing to ask what it should do . <p> In 1966 , for instance , researchers at Stanford University wrote a paper that envisaged using a computer program similar to Weizenbaum 's as a form of psychotherapy . " Because of the time-sharing capabilities of modern and future computers , several hundred patients an hour could be handled by a computer system designed for this purpose , " they wrote . <p> Weizenbaum was appalled . He thought there were " some human functions for which computers ought not to be substituted . It has nothing to do with what computers can or can not be made to do , " he added . " Respect , understanding and love are not technical problems . " <p> In his book , which turned him into a self-described " heretic " in the field of computer science , he argued that the most important issues were " neither technological nor even mathematical ; they are ethical . They can not be settled by asking questions beginning with ' can ' . The limits of the applicability @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> Half a century later , a strange hush has descended over the labour market in many countries -- not least the US , where artificial intelligence has expanded from the nascent field it was when Weizenbaum was writing in the 1970s to a sector that has propelled the stock market to record highs . <p> Yet , notwithstanding the splashy headlines every few days about tech companies making AI-driven lay-offs , very little is actually happening in the labour market overall . The rate at which people are getting fired is low . The rate at which people are getting hired is low . The rate at which people are quitting their jobs is low . It 's as if everyone is holding their breath and waiting for what they have been told is coming . <p> Last month , Mat Honan , the editor in chief of the MIT Technology Review , wrote an unusual , meditative essay titled The Era of AI Malaise that captured the mood . " It 's buying things while we sleep . It 's discovering the structure of proteins . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ telling children to kill themselves , " he wrote . " Will I have a job tomorrow ? Will the market crash ? Why does OpenAI need a bunker ? Do I need a bunker ? Maybe I should have a bunker . " <p> We are told that 40 per cent of the jobs in the world are exposed to AI ... Nobody wants to look like the fool who thinks you can hold back the tide . Photograph : iStock <p> And while we wait , we forecast . Endlessly , we forecast . Among government departments , universities , think tanks and AI labs , a cottage industry has grown up in producing technical mapping exercises , many of which use large language models themselves to compare their capabilities against all the tasks that supposedly go into each person 's job , in order to predict which are most " exposed " to automation . <p> Never mind that these predictions vary a lot depending on which model is used . And never mind that " exposed " does n't tell you anything about whether your job @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , or worse , or disappear altogether . <p> And so we are told that 40 per cent of the jobs in the world are exposed to AI ; or maybe it 's 300 million ; or perhaps only 92 million jobs are really in trouble . <p> Weizenbaum would not be surprised by this obsession with forecasts . Back in the 1970s , he already saw that people were becoming distracted by the " unnecessary , interminable and ultimately sterile exercise of making a catalogue of what computers will and will not be able to do " . <p> The metaphors we use are part of the problem . It is often said that a " wave " or " tsunami " of technological change is heading our way ( indeed , I have written in these terms myself in the past ) . Mustafa Suleyman , chief executive of Microsoft AI , wrote a bestselling book called The Coming Wave . According to the book by Quinn Slobodian and Ben Tarnoff , Elon Musk once described his " Doge " efforts to overhaul the US government as akin @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trash ... But how much does cleaning the beach really matter if you 've got a thousand-foot tsunami about to hit ? " <p> Metaphors are powerful because they shape our sense of what seems reasonable and unreasonable . What does the metaphor of AI as a tsunami imply would be a sensible course of action ? To forecast -- certainly . To map out the contours of where it is going to hit hardest ; who it 's going to affect ; who needs to prepare . What else ? To get ready to mop up afterwards : to compensate those whose livelihoods have been swept away . <p> And what does it imply would be an unreasonable response ? To think you have any say over the wave itself : its pace ; its trajectory ; what it should be allowed to do and not allowed to do . Nobody wants to look like the fool who thinks you can hold back the tide . These metaphors also cast technology company chief executives in the role of earnest weathermen , wanting to let people know what is coming @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> But technological change is not analogous to a natural phenomenon . How technology changes the world is shaped by institutions , laws , consumer demand , regulation , management strategies and the balance of power in different workplaces . This is not an aspirational statement , it is a simple matter of fact . <p> Why are there more radiologists now than there were in 2016 when Geoffrey Hinton , the " godfather of AI " , said we should stop training new ones because AI could already do their jobs ? Because the expertise and daily work of a radiologist is much more than just reading scans . Because when some types of products or services become cheaper to provide , thanks to a productivity-boosting new technology , demand for them simply expands . Because insurance companies are nervous about insuring fully autonomous systems that -- if they develop a fault -- can make mistakes at a pace and volume that a radiologist having a bad day never could . <p> Why are some local journalists in Cleveland , Ohio , being told that AI will now write @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- work for an organisation that has said its articles will continue to be written by humans ? Because our companies have different business models , different people at the top , different customers to please . <p> What this implies is that the space between what the machines can and ca n't do , and how the world does and does n't change , is a vast one -- and one that will only be filled in by a series of human decisions , arguments , markets , battles , compromises and preferences . <p> I have spent the past few years working on a book about people on the front lines of technological change , from truck drivers and warehouse workers to translators and Hollywood writers . One of the things that struck me most was that none of these people were standing paralysed as a " tsunami " washed over them . <p> As someone who escaped the Holocaust as a boy , Joseph Weizenbaum knew that it is a dangerous thing when people begin to see other people as ' non-player characters ' -- and even @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> When change turns from something abstract but looming to something real and present , people do n't just close their eyes and hope for the best . They do n't sit passively in the ruins of their former careers , waiting for the government to compensate them in some way . They take action , individually and collectively , to seize opportunities , or pivot away from risks and also , when circumstances allow , to actively shape the technology itself and the way it is implemented . <p> At the individual level , I met highly experienced professional translators who were angry and sad that machine translation technologies had stripped their work of meaning and pleasure : that much of the work , now , was to " post-edit " the translations of machines : to check and tidy them up , at twice the speed , for half the price . <p> But they were n't just accepting it . Some were moving into adjacent professions ; others were changing their own business models to sell their services directly to clients , rather than via agencies . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ she simply refused to see herself as a " loser " from technological change . " It takes away all our sense of agency , " she said . " I 'm just not willing to let that happen . I 'm going to try and fight for this . " <p> In the workplace too I witnessed examples of workers and employers navigating new technology in a way that was far removed from the " employer insistence/employee resistance " narrative that seems to be taking hold in some American companies such as Meta . In countries such as Sweden , for example , which has a workplace system based on the principle of " co-determination " between workers and employers , people have already got decades ' worth of muscle memory of how to navigate technological change . <p> Deep inside a mine below a Swedish pine forest on the fringe of the Arctic Circle , I witnessed how that was playing out in the AI era , in a highly productive workplace now populated by self-driving trucks . In one instance , management wanted to introduce a real-time @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The miners were worried about feeling surveilled , like Amazon warehouse workers . <p> So they came to a compromise : the mining company asked the technology vendor behind the system to develop anonymisation tags , so the company could see where everyone was , but not who they were ( except in the case of emergencies ) . <p> Unlike in many US and UK companies , where there is a growing sense of mistrust between employers and workers over AI , the miners I met in Sweden had a seat at the table when it came to its implementation , and so felt less fearful about it . One told me : " We are not so technology-hostile . We are more friendly to doing new things . We see the possibilities . " <p> In the realm of local and national politics , too , people have begun to shake off the feeling of paralysis and ask questions that begin with " ought " . <p> In the US , communities are protesting against new data centres . Individual states have been trying to regulate AI systems @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ realm of chatbots and child safety . <p> It should be no surprise that the safety of children has become one of the first issues to cut through . It was the same in the industrial revolution , where some of the early Factory Acts were attempts to prevent young children having to work long shifts in the new mills . Then , as now , even a society bewildered and disorientated by the pace of change was able to stir itself eventually to protect its young . <p> Indeed , the people who still seem most mesmerised are those who are supposedly at the steering wheel . It is tempting to suspect that tech workers in places such as Silicon Valley see themselves as masters of the universe and everyone else as " non-player characters " , as the bro-culture insult puts it -- a term from the gaming world to describe characters in the background in computer games , who are just there to further your own game or add a bit of atmosphere . But the truth is stranger than that . <p> In Silicon Valley , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Stop hiring humans " and " That 's so agentic " , many people believe they are working on humanity 's last invention -- that once AI reaches a tipping point , it will embark on recursive self-improvement , getting better and better on its own . <p> And once that happens , some think the social structure will become fixed : that there will be some winners and many losers . In a piece last month for The New York Times called Silicon Valley Is Bracing for a Permanent Underclass , the San Francisco-based writer Jasmine Sun 's first line was : " Most people I know in the AI industry think the median person is screwed , and they have no idea what to do about it . " <p> There is something telling about the word " permanent " , which implies a lack of faith in politics to address inequality of opportunity or outcome . That is perhaps no surprise , given the current state of US politics . But I suspect it also runs deeper . In 1992 , the cultural critic Neil Postman wrote @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ information technologies were hastening the future Weizenbaum had worried about . Technopoly , Postman wrote , " is what happens when a culture , overcome by information generated by technology , tries to employ technology itself as a means of providing clear direction and humane purpose " . <p> If anywhere is surrendering to the ideology of technopoly , it is Silicon Valley -- a place where people are racing towards " superintelligent " and " agentic " machines that will supposedly be able to solve all humanity 's problems for us , from poverty to climate change , but might also render a lot of people unemployable , or , in fact , just kill everyone . Tech leaders tell us they do n't know for sure which way it will go . All they know is that they ca n't stop . <p> Last month , Pope Leo issued an encyclical on AI titled " Magnifica humanitas " in which he wrote that although " not everyone has the same power to make a difference " , still " no one is without responsibility " to shape the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , one of the co-founders of AI company Anthropic . Someone , you might have thought , who has rather a lot of power to make a difference . But he pleaded with the rest of the world to step in . <p> " Every frontier AI lab -- including Anthropic -- operates inside a set of incentives and constraints that can sometimes conflict with doing the right thing , " he said . " The pressure to stay commercially viable and to stay at the research frontier . Geopolitical pressure . And the older , plainer pressures of pride and ambition . <p> " No matter how sincerely any of us intend to do the right thing -- and I believe many of us do -- we will always be influenced by those incentives ... We need informed critics who will tell the labs when we are failing . We need moral voices that the incentives can not bend . " <p> In his 1976 book , Weizenbaum wrote about something he called " civil courage " . " Every individual must act as if the whole future of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Anything less is a shirking of responsibility and is itself a dehumanising force , for anything less encourages the individual to look upon himself as a mere actor in a drama written by anonymous agents . " <p> That might sound a little grandiose for a book that is , at least ostensibly , mostly about computer programming . But as someone who escaped the Holocaust as a boy , Weizenbaum knew that it is a dangerous thing when people begin to see other people as " non-player characters " -- and even more dangerous if they begin to see themselves that way . <p> My sense is that the era of " AI malaise " is coming to an end -- at least , everywhere except the place that is supposedly in charge of it . Admittedly , trying to shape the application of technology through human debate , compromise and action can be wearying and effortful . Participating in politics , at the level of the workplace , the community or the nation , can be frequently enraging and unsatisfying . But it is better than watching @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ future , when even they do n't seem to think they can be trusted with it . -- Copyright The Financial Times Limited 2026

@@137848631 <h> The joyless World Cup : From sportswashing to foreboding <p> For one glorious month every four years , football asks us to ignore the noise of the likes of wars , human rights abuses , politics , climate change and economic crises outside the stadium , writes Colin Sheridan <p> FIFA World Cup sign at the New York New Jersey Stadium in the US . Photo : Andrew Milligan/PA <p> " Modern societies deny men and women the experience of solidarity , which football provides to the point of collective delirium . Most car mechanics and shop assistants feel shut out by high culture ; but once a week they bear witness to displays of sublime artistry by men for whom the word genius is sometimes no mere hype . Like a jazz band or drama company , football blends dazzling individual talent with selfless teamwork , thus solving a problem over which sociologists have long agonised . Co-operation and competition are cunningly balanced . Blind loyalty and internecine rivalry gratify some of our most powerful evolutionary instincts . " <p> -- From Football , a dear friend to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ always demanded a suspension of disbelief . For one glorious month every four years , football asks us to ignore the noise outside the stadium . <p> Wars can wait . Human Rights abuses can take a seat . Politics can join the virtual queue . Disappeared migrant workers , catastrophic climate change , economic crises -- all are temporarily pushed to the margins as the world gathers around a single ball . <p> It is , to mix a bunch of metaphors , the greatest trick FIFA has ever pulled : Convincing us all that , for five weeks at least , the world is a fine place and worth fighting for . <p> Six days out from the latest instalment , that sentiment seems as remote as world peace . One host country , the US , is perpetuating an on-again , off-again war with one of the tournament 's participants , Iran . <p> It has also threatened violent interventions on Mexico and Colombia , annexation of Canada , and a sweeping " you 're next " to any country that dared qualify for the World Cup @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with tournaments staged against deeply uncomfortable backdrops . Argentina hosted in 1978 , while a military junta disappeared tens of thousands of its own citizens . <p> Russia used the 2018 World Cup to project an image of modernity and competence to the outside world . Qatar spent more than a decade defending itself against accusations of labour exploitation , migrant worker abuse and sportswashing before producing -- perhaps accidentally -- one of the most memorable tournaments in history . <p> Yet all three hosts shared something important . They wanted the world to like them . That , more than anything , is what makes the 2026 World Cup feel different . <p> What makes the 2026 World Cup different <p> As the tournament kicks off across the US , Canada , and Mexico , football 's greatest spectacle arrives amid a backdrop of geopolitical instability , trade disputes , immigration crackdowns , Washington-sponsored wars in the Middle East and an American administration seemingly incapable of separating governance from deeply offensive performance art . <p> For veteran American sportswriter Dave Zirin , who has spent decades chronicling the intersection @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for a surprisingly simple reason . <p> " What 's distinct is how joyless it is , " he says . " In previous World Cups , even in ones staged in autocracies , there was a sportswashing element where countries wanted to show off for the world . <p> The only thing the US is showing off is its narcissistic president , violent immigration controls , and a sense of foreboding . <p> That observation cuts to the heart of a tournament that increasingly feels less like a celebration than a reflection of the world that produced it . So , there is no " washing " then , no attempt to purify a sullied reputation . More , bathing in the outrage Trump gleefully provokes . <p> The notion that football exists separately from politics has always been a convenient fiction . <p> Uruguay 's inaugural World Cup in 1930 was a statement of national identity . Italy 's victory under Mussolini in 1934 became a propaganda triumph for fascism . <p> Colombia fans outside the New York New Jersey Stadium in the US . Photo : Andrew @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of hosting in 1978 . Vladimir Putin certainly understood it in 2018 , especially in the context of what was to come . <p> The World Cup has never existed outside politics . What has changed is the willingness of FIFA to maintain the pretence , and even lean into it . <p> Under Gianni Infantino , the organisation has become increasingly comfortable aligning itself with political power wherever it resides . The sight of Mr Infantino cultivating a close relationship with Donald Trump has reinforced the perception that football 's governing body is no longer interested in appearing neutral . <p> " Gianni Infantino 's FIFA has dropped all fig leaves , " says Mr Zirin . " He has declared himself to be an explicit handmaiden to right-wing authoritarianism . " <p> Strong words , certainly , but impossible to dismiss . <p> This is , after all , the same FIFA president who awarded Mr Trump the FIFA Peace Prize last year , praising his efforts to promote peace and unity while Palestinian babies were being slaughtered with America 's blessing , and while Lebanon was being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ routinely stabbed for fun . <p> US President Donald Trump is presented with the inaugural FIFA Peace Prize award by FIFA President Gianni Infantino during the 2026 FIFA World Cup draw last December . Photo : Sam Corum/PA <p> Such grotesque contradictions have become impossible to ignore . <p> The US enters this tournament not merely as a host nation but as an active participant in some of the most horrific humanitarian crises of the age . Its unwavering support for Israel 's war in Gaza has generated condemnation across much of the world , if little by way of meaningful push-back . <p> Relations with Iran remain excruciatingly volatile . Even the supposedly harmonious partnership between co-hosts Canada , Mexico and the US has been strained by trade disputes and nationalist rhetoric . <p> All of this unfolds while FIFA continues to market the tournament as a universal celebration capable of transcending political divisions . <p> The tension between those competing realities has been the defining narrative of the build-up to this tournament . Not the health of Harry Kane or the form of Lionel Messi . <p> Yet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it is worth asking whether that scrutiny is being applied evenly . <p> The Qatar World Cup <p> The last World Cup in Qatar generated an extraordinary level of attention around labour rights , migrant workers and the kafala system . <p> Critics argued that Doha was using football to launder its international reputation . Defenders countered that sustained scrutiny forced meaningful reforms that might otherwise never have occurred . <p> The truth appears to lie somewhere in between . <p> Under intense international pressure , Qatar introduced a series of labour reforms in advance of the tournament . Elements of the kafala sponsorship system were dismantled , a non-discriminatory minimum wage was introduced , and workers were granted greater freedom to change employers without requiring permission from sponsors . <p> Those changes mattered . <p> Yet one year after the final , Amnesty International reported that progress had largely stalled . Wage theft remained widespread , access to justice remained inconsistent , and enforcement mechanisms weakened as global attention shifted elsewhere . <p> Academic assessments have reached similar conclusions : the World Cup accelerated reform , but many @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the tournament rather than a permanent transformation of the labour system . <p> The lesson is not that criticism of Qatar was misplaced . Rather , it is that mega-events often generate temporary reform without necessarily producing lasting structural change . <p> The cameras leave . The pressure subsides . Old habits return . <p> This is precisely why comparisons between Qatar and the United States are useful -- not because the two countries are identical , but because they reveal the inconsistency with which host nations are often judged . <p> Qatar 's failings were relentlessly scrutinised . America 's are more likely to be framed as domestic political disputes rather than human rights questions . <p> Yet both examples point toward the same uncomfortable reality : FIFA repeatedly awards its flagship tournament to states whose political interests frequently collide with the values the organisation claims to represent . <p> Mr Zirin agrees that liberal democracies have often benefited from a different standard . <p> " Liberal democracies get a pass on their myriad injustices come World Cup time , " he says . " But the US is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a neoconfederate Supreme Court , all pretences of being a liberal democracy are gone . " <p> World Cup opposition <p> That scrutiny is already evident on the ground . <p> Writing in The Nation , journalist Brian Dolinar documented how opposition to the tournament is taking shape across several host cities . <p> Their concern was not merely undocumented migrants , but foreign visitors with valid travel documentation potentially becoming entangled in a system that has grown increasingly punitive and unpredictable . <p> FIFA World Cup sign at the New York New Jersey Stadium in the US . Photo : Andrew Milligan/PA <p> In Kansas City , activists have mobilised against the construction of what they call a " World Cup jail " -- a detention facility critics argue is being fast-tracked ahead of the tournament . <p> Campaigners fear the project will ultimately be used to criminalise homelessness and remove vulnerable people from public view as the city prepares for an influx of visitors . <p> Los Angeles has become another flashpoint . Community organisers there continue to draw parallels with the 1996 Olympic Games , when preparations for the event were accompanied by displacement , gentrification and aggressive policing measures . <p> They fear that history may repeat itself under a different banner . <p> These disputes are not unique to the US . <p> From Rio to London , Beijing to Paris , major sporting events have long been associated with gentrification , securitisation and the transformation of public space . <p> But the scale and diversity of opposition emerging around the 2026 World Cup suggest something deeper is at work . <p> For Mr Zirin , mega-events do not merely expose existing inequalities . They amplify them . <p> " I 've been covering the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The tournament does not merely expose existing inequalities . " <p> That observation may prove especially significant in a tournament spread across 16 host cities and three countries . <p> Unlike Qatar , which concentrated its spectacle within a relatively compact geographic footprint , the 2026 World Cup will stretch across a continent . Its logistical demands are unprecedented . So too are its security requirements . <p> The result is a tournament that increasingly resembles a vast exercise in management and control . @@137849113 <p> MIT researchers have developed a new electrochemical approach that could make carbon dioxide capture more energy-efficient and scalable , offering an alternative to conventional amine-based systems that are widely used today but remain costly and power-intensive . <p> Carbon capture is considered a key tool in climate change mitigation , but current technologies struggle with high energy demands and limited scalability . The most common industrial method , amine scrubbing , requires significant heat input to separate CO2 from gas streams , making it difficult to deploy widely despite growing emissions reduction targets . <p> To address these limitations , researchers at MIT are exploring electrochemically mediated CO2 capture ( EMCC ) , a method that uses electricity instead of heat to drive gas separation . The approach is designed to integrate with renewable energy systems , potentially lowering the overall carbon footprint of the capture process . <p> However , EMCC systems have their own challenges . Many rely on sorbents that operate under highly reducing conditions , which can trigger unwanted oxygen reduction reactions . These side reactions reduce efficiency and can degrade long-term system performance , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the MIT team investigated a new class of sorbents called N-heterocyclic imines ( NHIs ) . The researchers say these molecules can be chemically tuned and may enable more stable and efficient electrochemical CO2 separation without requiring extreme operating conditions . <p> " Our work translates these NHIs for the first time into the EMCC application space , and demonstrates that NHI-based sorbents can be modulated electrochemically for CO2 separation by a unique separation mechanism that avoids the need of applying highly reducing potentials . " <p> The team focused on a bis(NHI) structure that could theoretically enable the capture of two CO2 molecules per electron during operation , potentially improving electron efficiency in the system . The researchers also suggest that further molecular engineering could enhance CO2 binding strength and allow the system to function across a wider range of electrolyte environments . <h> Molecular design shift <p> The study highlights how small molecular changes could influence large-scale carbon capture performance . By tuning the structure of bis(NHI) compounds , the researchers believe it may be possible to improve both energy efficiency and operational flexibility in EMCC systems . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ gaining deeper mechanistic insight into the stability and degradation pathways of the bis(NHI) radical cation . " <p> Understanding these degradation pathways will be essential for improving durability , one of the main barriers to real-world deployment of electrochemical carbon capture technologies . <p> The approach is still at an early stage , but it points toward a possible route for reducing the energy penalty associated with capturing CO2 from industrial emissions or directly from air . <h> Toward scalable capture <p> While conventional capture systems remain dominant , the MIT work suggests electrochemical alternatives could eventually offer a more flexible and renewable-powered pathway for carbon removal . The key challenge now lies in improving material stability and scaling the chemistry beyond laboratory conditions . <p> The researchers say further work will focus on optimizing molecular design to extend operational lifetimes and improve cycling performance , both of which are required for practical deployment . <h> Recommended Articles <p> With over a decade-long career in journalism , Neetika Walter has worked with The Economic Times , ANI , and Hindustan Times , covering politics , business , technology , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ books , poetry , and storytelling , she brings depth and insight to her writing . When she is n't chasing stories , she 's likely lost in a book or enjoying the company of her dogs . 
@@137864574 <p> Both farmers and the general public underestimate each other 's worry about climate change and willingness to take climate action , a recent study carried out by the Economic and Social Research Institute ( ESRI ) has found . <p> The objective of the study was to investigate climate misperceptions , comparing how farmers and non-farmers see their own , their peers ' and each other 's worry about climate change and willingness to take action . <p> In the survey , each of the three groups was asked to rate their own worry about climate change and their willingness to take climate action . <p> Participants were also asked to rate farmers and the general public using the same scales . <p> For the general public , climate actions involved various lifestyle changes , such as transport , energy and food choices , while for farmers it involved using climate-friendly farming methods and technologies . <p> Results <p> Both farmers and non-farmers expressed worry about climate change and willingness to take climate action . <p> All groups , except farmers themselves , underestimated farmers ' worry and willingness @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ willingness . <p> No evidence of an urban-rural divide in attitudes to climate change was found . <p> Individual 's perceptions of others climate views can be inaccurate due to " pluralistic ignorance " , which is when people collectively underestimate the majority 's views , the study details . <p> The study also found strong false consensus effects , meaning the more that people were worried and willing to act , the more they thought other people were and vice-versa . <p> This suggests that individual biases may be more important than collective misperceptions when it comes to climate . <p> Many individuals decide whether to co-operate on climate action based on whether they think others will too , the study highlights . <p> This study highlights some of the misperceptions that hinder climate action and the @@137870202 <h> An issue is making hydrangeas increasingly unsuitable for Irish gardens , with horticultural experts recommending alternatives such as lavender , ceanothus and Russian sage as more sustainable gardening plants for Ireland <p> For gardeners who are n't quite ready to wave goodbye to their vibrant blooms just yet , there 's an pressing call to seek out alternatives to hydrangeas while there 's still time . As the mercury rises , plants we treasure are facing significant challenges , and horticultural specialists suggest we bear this in mind when cultivating our favourite florals . <p> In a world where temperatures keep climbing , moisture-dependent varieties like hydrangeas are proving ill-suited to their increasingly parched surroundings . <p> Even in areas where these plants have historically flourished , they 're now succumbing to prolonged dry spells @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Echoing this worry , one nursery proprietor told the Farmiddable Observer : " I lost nearly 70% of my hydrangea collection during the 2022 heat waves despite daily watering . Furthermore , if you notice stems beginning to sag instead of standing tall , this is a definite warning sign that your plant is struggling badly . Horticultural experts are now more frequently advising clients to consider drought-tolerant options that still provide stunning colour without the hefty water requirements . <p> Plants like lavender , ceanothus and Russian sage are being put forward as excellent choices , all producing impressive displays of flowers while being far less demanding to maintain . <p> For added convenience , it 's recommended that gardeners position drought-resistant bulbs in traditionally shadier and moister areas to create a stronger garden foundation . <p> This could encompass autumn-blooming perennials and strategically timed spring plantings , best introduced @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ As horticultural practices evolve toward a more environmentally conscious approach , moving away from hydrangeas appears to be just the start . <p> By adjusting our planting choices , we 're not only acknowledging the changes in our climate , but we 're also helping to cut down on the amount of water used for plant care . <p> The time previously spent nurturing hydrangeas can instead be directed towards tending other plants that will flourish naturally in these conditions . <p> At Reach and across our entities we and our partners use information collected through cookies and other identifiers from your device to improve experience on our site , analyse how it is used and to show personalised advertising . @@137878974 <h> Earthbound : Clare Langan 's film installation gives us a future beyond the end of the world <p> Through a doorway off a courtyard that you reach via a lane beyond the busy streets of a hot , dusty Paris is a vast world of ice . Meltwater drips inside a glacial cave , where sharp facets glitter and shadows lurk , masking unknowable depths . Above , light leads outside , where the landscape is made up of jagged fissures in blue , white and grey , stretching to vanishing point . <p> " I 'm holding on to that possibility that there is something that can be done , " says the artist Clare Langan , whose most recent film , Earthbound , imagines the aftermath of a future ice age . <p> In her four-screen installation , which is premiering at the Irish Cultural Centre in the city , a young woman wakes up , as if from hibernation , to discover the melting glaciers and icefields revealing almost archaeological discoveries : preserved birds and plants , as well as human detritus , the artefacts of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the pandemic led Langan to realise that nature can regenerate . " There was a stillness . Certain insects came back . There was this element of rewilding , of regeneration and transformation . It is a possibility . " <p> The term Anthropocene , which entered common usage at the turn of the millennium , is used to characterise our current era , in which humanity has become the primary force of planetary change . <p> " There are a lot more artists talking about it now , " Langan says . What has altered for her is the inclusion of that possibility for hope . <p> Made from 1999 onwards , the award-winning artist 's film trilogy Forty Below , Too Dark for Night and The Glass Hour explored the effects of climate change , always with the teasing and intelligent ambiguity that characterises her work . <p> That trilogy , now in the collection of the Irish Museum of Modern Art , shows , respectively , the impacts of a vast lava flow , encroaching desert and deluge . The works cause ideas to form in your @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is an element of bleakness to them that 's absent from Earthbound . <p> " You ca n't go round being the horseman of the apocalypse , " Langan says as we talk outside in the spring sunshine of Paris . " As humans we 're resilient , and resilience and hope go hand in hand . You get knocked down , you stand up . " <p> Clare Langan , Earthbound , at Centre Culturel Irlandais <p> Alongside the hope there is beauty , as Langan 's awakened figure runs her hand in wonder across the ice , then , reaching for the light , emerges into the whitened world . <p> The artist has been thinking about fossils emerging from preverbal , prehistorical times , their origins and stories pieced together by scholars . <p> " I was looking at how much we can preserve , and see and learn from them , and then imagined what it is we 're leaving behind for a future explorer to find , with our plastic that is not going to disappear , and our animals that are already extinct . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Part of the brilliance of Langan 's always haunting work is that balance of her meticulously professional and intelligent approach with her admission of emotion . Her aesthetics are exceptional , but there is far more to her films and installations than surface beauty . <p> As the four elements of Earthbound play around you , you are immersed in its world but also sense that something is happening just out of view . There are fleeting glimpses , the mesmerisingly exquisite feathers of a frozen bird , an umbrella , a plastic bottle , a light bulb , held as if in amber but instead in ice . <p> " The human character adds a sense of relationship and emotion . It signifies our place in the world , " Langan says . " The cave is claustrophobic , but then she 's tiny in the landscape . " <p> Born in Dublin and now based in Co Kerry , Langan studied at the National College of Art and Design before a Fulbright scholarship brought her to complete a film course at New York University . Returning to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that included Braveheart , Far and Away and Some Mother 's Son before realising that art was where her true passion lay . <p> " It was an amazing education , because I saw how teams worked together , " she says . <p> She still has close friends from those days , and a core collaborator from the outset has been cinematographer Robbie Ryan , whose film credits include Poor Things and The Favourite . " A producer introduced us . He was this enthusiastic , experimental guy . We bought a Bolex camera and used my hand-painted filters in front of the lens . <p> " I 'm lucky , " Langan says , " that he still wants to come on my shoots . He calls them my ' boot camps ' , because they are really hard . " <p> She laughs , describing the process of filming in Iceland , the tiny team trekking up the glacier and into the ice cave , everyone helping to lug the equipment . <p> " Robbie is brilliant , and that 's the thing about film : it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> In this instance , " everyone " also includes the editor Adam Finch , who is best known for his work with the British artist and film-maker Isaac Julien ; the composers Gyda Valt ? sd ? ttir and ? lfur Hansson ; and the curator Heinz Peter Schwerfel . <p> Back in the darkened exhibition space at the Irish Cultural Centre , a small bubble of ice looks like an alien face , another a sleeping creature . A whole world of wonder appears in a single droplet of water . The human figure holds up a torch , or perhaps it 's a smartphone . <p> Perspectives shift and scale alters . The figure climbs ; she drinks the meltwater , then drinks in the daylight . Sounds move about . Patterns in the ice look like hieroglyphics , their hues set against the warm tones of living skin . A handprint could be a future fossil being formed . We can not help but leave a trace . <p> The red silk of the figure 's cloak is echoed in the feathers of a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ beacon -- or perhaps a warning that humanity is coming back . <p> While Langan filmed on location in Iceland , she also created ice sculptures back at her home studio , where she has two freezers , one for food and one for some of the subjects of her work . <p> " That one can be a bit of a horror show , " she says , smiling , and I 'm reminded that the late artist and film-maker David Lynch always said he had something similar going on in his freezer at home . <p> " We find what drives us , " Langan says . " It changes over time , but I am very immersed in what I do . " <p> She is driven but knows herself well enough to realise when to leaven things . When not on location , she spends mornings intensely working , while afternoons bring walks , which she describes as " my balancing point " . <p> The Ireland Invites programme put together by the Irish Museum of Modern Art , the Hugh Lane and Culture Ireland brings @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an international guest curator every two years , but Ireland is nonetheless a small country , geographically on the periphery of Europe . <p> Showing in Paris gives Langan the chance to connect with larger networks . She has already represented Ireland at international biennales and this is work that deserves to be seen on the widest of stages . <p> Since Forty Below , more than 25 years ago , her work has had an increasing urgency , and the conversations it creates are ones with which more and more artists and writers are becoming involved . <p> " It is shocking and sad , " Langan says , considering the state of the planet . " And there is so much going on you would really feel like this is the end of it . But I think you can not . That 's part of the human spirit . It 's part of the soul .
